ParlaMint-PL_2016-01-28-sejm-09-2.u305	At one point, it occurred to me that some of the compliments I heard weren't entirely honest. It turned out that they were completely dishonest. I think you should read this document in a specific way. This is government information about government work, actually two governments over the six-month period. If, therefore, the State refers to the wording of this information which refers to the implementation of the government's position or to one or another of the government's positions, it must be borne in mind that it refers to the government's position at a given point in time. It would be difficult for government information from the work to contain polemic threads and some ex post evaluations. This is just a register of cases that were taken by individual offices. It is a fact, perhaps some in this Chamber, but the fact is that Poland's membership of the European Union is covered by a relatively broad political consensus, in particular on individual sectoral issues. It seems to me that it builds the strength of our European policy rather than some weakness or weakness. In many regulatory areas - this has sounded in this debate, I think very clearly - such as the common market, the policy towards the East, I hope that also migration policy, the solution to migration, we will be able to create, expand Poland's consensus so that the Polish position is as strong as possible in the European forum. It is usually the case that countries that succeed in the European Union are those that do not engage in radical polemics in terms of their objectives towards the European Union. These are comments on the framework of this debate. I don't think it would be good for us - especially by force - to look for elements that Polish politics and its continuity should overwhelm. This does not mean that political change in Poland is meant to mean total continuity, no changes in the area of relations between Warsaw and Brussels. Some of the voices in this debate actually referred to the undisclosed dissatisfiedness of this change, suggesting some very aggregated opinions about whether we are enthusiasts or skeptics. It seems to me that this goes beyond the scope of this discussion, because this is a meta-discussion that goes on from time to time in some parts of Europe. I think that when we talk in Parliament about a very specific report on the activity of the country in the European Union, we should focus on the concrete and not look there for confirmation of some very, very general hypothesis. To put it simply, there are things in the European Union that I am very enthusiastic about, and there are things that I am very skeptical about. This does not consist in any aggregate view that would prejudice the position of the 100 different cases that will appear before us. It seems to me that such a policy of predefined position, let us say that we will always be satisfied or always dissatisfied, was a symptom of stupidity of Polish politics rather than refining it. I think that refining Polish European policy is an important element and should be a valuable dimension, because Poland has no reason to reduce its position in some tacky way to a shallow yes or no. Poland has 100 different matters to attend to in the European Union, Poland must present a position which will be well understood by our partners and, of course, make very clear what we like and what we do not. It seems to me that the whole sense of law and justice policy for a very long time, starting with the accession referendum, was precisely to break up such aggregate, generalized, shallow behaviour towards the European Union or towards Europe. It also seems to me that the maturity of the countries of the European Union is that they are able to say more about the European Union than yes or no, either they like it or they don't like it. There are different issues in the European Union, there are those that are added value to our policy, there are those that are not added value to our policy, and the country, a mature country, must be able to do so simply. One of the dimensions of changes throughout the European Union, but also in Poland, is probably the growing scepticism about any central, bureaucratic attempts from the level of Brussels, in addition with very specific procedures, saying the most comprehensive, not transparent, management of the migration crisis. I think it doesn't take long to find answers to the question whether we've dealt with this crisis. The migration crisis is an unsolved crisis in the European Union. These words, this opinion, are not addressed to the Luxembourg Presidency, because no Presidency, in fact, as an organ that organises the work of the Union, will be able to cope with this existential, complicated, very serious, in my opinion, with the biggest crisis we have had so far in Europe. It's a responsibility. All of us. Contrary to what people think, whose opinions are extremely shallow about the construction of the European Union, policies in the European Union are made up of Member States, at least those who want to speak with their own voice and clearly what they want. This process is not suspended in some political vacuum, it does not depend on the Presidency, it does not even depend on the European Commission, it is increasingly - we can have one or another relationship - it depends on the active countries that want to take matters into their own hands. Poland today is a country that wants to take matters into its own hands. To answer the question about active migration policies, I can say that I think you can see it with the naked eye. The debut of the new government and the debut of Prime Minister Beata Szydło at the European Council level took place at the occasion of the extraordinary summit of the European Union and Turkey, and was continued at the regular summit in December. Both of these summits respond to our own expectations in the area of solving the migration problem. Both of these summits have actually been very firmly rooted in the agenda of the European Union, not a new one, but a very fundamental view that we are close to solving the migration crisis. The first issue is cooperation with third countries at source. This is serious. I'll get back to that when I comment on Turkey. The second issue, of course, is to strengthen the external border, which is important not only for restoring control of migration flows, but also for the survival of the Schengen area. We can laugh at the fact that politicians today who are talking about mini-Schengen do not have a clear conscience about this, because some politicians have never really wanted a broad Union, did not want a broad Schengen and today is taking advantage of the opportunity to warm up this topic. But fears, fears, doubts of society as it were, the cores of those countries that are deeper within the European Union and do not in fact manage, with the exception of airports, the external borders, the concerns of the Dutch or Germany societies as to whether this state of control of the external border gives rise to the maintenance of the Schengen area, are fully justified. We can't go sideways and acknowledge that we want Schengen because we want to. If we want Schengen, we need to prove that we are actually committed, constructively committed to ensuring that the external border is protected. Today, happily, the border that broke is not the border for which we are directly responsible. But that doesn't mean it's completely neutral from our point of view. The fact that the southern border is in the state it is in today is also a Polish concern. Therefore, from the beginning Poland offered technical assistance, already realized, from the beginning it offered financial assistance, also to a large extent implemented. Soon, on 4 February, Prime Minister Beata Szydło is going to attend the Donor Conference on the Fund for Syria for exactly the same purpose, to repeat the Polish position on this matter. We are very committed, very ready to cooperate constructively at EU level in these two areas, while we are equally strongly opposed to relocation mechanisms or any other type of mechanism. We believe that Member States should control their migration policies, their refugee policies. That doesn't mean we're going to have immigration and refugee policy in the form of one big zero somewhere on the horizon. That would be absurd, let's face it. Poland has been leading a responsible, narrow but refugee policy for 25 years because we understand that we have humanitarian obligations towards those who really need international protection. Poland is leading, and will probably lead an even wider immigration policy, because these are the circumstances of the labour market in Europe, also in Poland. This is our own intention and no one has to force us to do so, while Poland will not agree to mechanisms that de facto limit the possibility of applying national instruments to implement this policy. In this sense, Poland retains all existing refugee policy instruments by answering questions about criteria. It has the right, and will exercise this right, to shape criteria for admission of persons, including as part of the relocation system, according to its own preferences, according to its own beliefs, according to its own view of the situation we are dealing with in the Middle East. This is an unprecedented situation even for those countries that have a huge migratory experience. There have never been so many people at the gates of Europe or in Europe alone who today are applying for refugee status and are applying for international protection. So far, we have been used everywhere in Europe to give this kind of status to individuals, about whom we know exactly what their condition is. Today we are dealing with this kind of expectation from huge groups of people. This is a situation for which Europe is not prepared. What we also communicate to our Western partners is that we all, even those countries that are most experienced in this area, cannot cope with this scale. This probably requires other means. As far as Turkey is concerned, contrary to what has been said here, no one has paid EUR 3 billion for Turkey, and therefore Poland has not paid any EUR 70 million for Turkey. This mechanism is a little more complicated. We have declared our willingness to implement such a plan if we have an offer and a factual basis to believe that Turkey will fulfil its responsibilities. The fact is that Europe has lost its ability to control the external border in the Aegean Sea. This is very sad news because we promised each other that we would do it, but unfortunately we can't do it. This means that we need to become, say, partners - I think, in some eyes of customers - third countries that are more or less willing to approach this type of offer and try to exploit it and, at the same time, deal with various interests. There is nothing unusual about this, this is a natural situation, if we are weakening, and in this case we have shown weakness, which is visible to the naked eye in every corner of the world. Therefore, I think that there is no reason to resent the fact that there has been an agreement with Turkey, a preliminary agreement which also includes a whole range of control mechanisms, mechanisms which determine not only the payment of these funds, which are intended to address specific needs - not the means for any use - but also the conditioning, warming of the political process. In this sense, I consider this agreement to be sensible, balanced and safe, also from a Polish point of view. Today a lot of people want to assess this agreement after a few weeks and say that it just doesn't work. I hope this is not true and I would also advise those who have such an opinion on the agreement with Turkey today to wait for just a moment. I think it takes time. In fact, the right point, the moment when we measure whether this agreement has given or not, is really going to be spring when immigration movements will not be stopped by atmospheric conditions. As far as questions are concerned about the very specific issues that have already been raised here, the north-south corridor. This is obviously the corridor north-south linking the Baltic, the North Sea, the Adriatic. This is a large project, where there are elements that are probably on the European Union's priority investment list. This is a project which, from a Polish point of view, we are discussing this on the day of the President of Croatia's visit to Poland, is a promising project, because it can mean, if it were carried out in transport terms, but in particular in terms of connection of energy systems, the possibility of creating a corridor north-south also for energy raw materials. This is an essential element. The reference point for this project is, of course, Polish LNG. Detailed elements concerning waterways are not known to me, but we will provide such information by written means, as well as information about digitization, digital libraries. In the Operational Programme, of course, such elements are digital Poland, whereas details must be checked by the specific resort responsible for this. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs is not directly involved in this undertaking. Migration at European level. I feel like I've answered what's going on here. As far as experience is concerned, there's obviously a natural reaction, right. If we have a problem, we feel that we have clearly less experience in this area than in other countries. Therefore, what is usually suggested in such situations is the exchange of experience. But let us be honest, without excluding the exchange of experience with this or another country, there are no good examples in Europe, there is no good experience in managing this problem. Countries that have been involved in this process for a very long time have had time not only to learn from their own mistakes, but have had a lot of time to review the basis of their policies and failed in this matter. The sense of failure of immigration policy, which was initially treated as a technocratic trick related to the labour market, is visible to the naked eye. Something went wrong in this case and it seems to me that Poland is in a very comfortable position to learn from these mistakes. These are the reasons why the Polish position, very skeptical towards such a built immigration policy, is increasingly understood in Europe. On the fundamental issue of European policy, we said that we should lean on Germany rather than build a second speed in Visegrad. Let me say this: Poland is too big and too mature a country to reduce its European policy to one or the other country, whether it is Germany or Hungary. We see the whole map of the European Union and it is a bit more spacious. There's no reason again to define your position and impose some limits on yourself in this area. The Union is richer. Poland obviously understands the importance of Germany, especially in our region, for the future of the European Union. We also understand the importance and lack of representation of the region of Central Europe, broadly understood, from Tallinn to Sofia, in the decision-making and political process of the European Union, but please do not forcefully suggest that we want to reduce Polish politics here or there. Poland will remain open to all Member States because we have very different political synergies on very different issues. I don't think there's really any reason to impose such limits. Two words about TTIP. It has nothing to do with the Presidency, this case lasts longer, it will last for a very long time. TTIP is an agreement to which the European Union and the United States will be parties. This does not change the fact that due to the density of this agreement - it goes far beyond the purely commercial agreement, it will also create regulatory standards, which will also apply in Poland - we will have to deal with, you will have to do with the national ratification of this agreement. In this sense, I think you can have some sense of security of this process and not expect any excessive surprises at the moment when it will be concluded, because Poland must necessarily be included in the whole process. Otherwise, we'd risk the ratification failure of this deal, and that, I get the feeling, nobody wants to. As a rule, Poland is interested in important, intense links between Europe and the United States, because the unity of the Western world this is an important thing for Poland. It's a condition of prosperity, it's also a condition of security. ParlaMint-PL_2016-01-28-sejm-09-2.u309	Mrs. Marshal! High Seym! The project is not long, so my justification will not take much time. I represent citizens who notice dissonance in Polish law, dissonance regarding state funding of the activities of churches and religious unions. I represent the citizens who have decided to give up this dissonance of consideration to the High House. When religion was introduced into schools 25 years ago, it was a gesture aimed at the previous system fighting against religion. This act was an attempt to make amends to the undeniable damage suffered by the communities of believers, especially the faithful of the Catholic Church, during the period of the Polish People's Republic. However, the model agreed at that time did not bear any particular burden on the taxpayer. Primate Józef Glemp himself declared: The Church does not want any zloty from the state budget for teaching religion in public schools. We don't go to school for money, we go there on a mission. This was the case in the early years of catechesis. The church was present in schools with its mission of forming believers, did not claim greater rights than would result from the guaranteed freedom of religion in Poland. It's different today. Today, catechesis stands for 30,000. employed workers from the so-called. the mission, that is in practice excluded from probation supervision, implementing programs which are not approved or controlled by the Polish State financing the entire project. Art. Article 12 (1) (a) and (b) of Regulation (EU) No 1303/2013 The 2nd concordat signed with the Holy See is simply: The program of teaching the Catholic religion and textbooks is developed by the church authority and communicated to the competent state authority. According to the signed international agreement, the Polish state has no influence on the program of the subject taught at the Polish school. The Republic of Poland cannot assume responsibility for the shape of religious lessons in the school according to the concordat. According to the regulations of the concordat, the Polish state has committed itself to organizing religious lessons in schools, but there is no record of a financial obligation. If he were, we would feel that he would be unconstitutional, contrary to the principle of worldview impartiality of the state enshrined in the article. The 25 constitution, which states: Public authorities in the Republic of Poland retain impartiality in matters of religious, worldview and philosophical beliefs, ensuring freedom to express them in public life. Neither the concordat nor the constitution imposes a obligation on the Polish state to finance the costs of catechesis. Raising the record of free education by the opponents of our project is a misunderstanding - it cannot be regarded as public education for activities not covered by the State's curriculum, excluded from the methodological and teaching supervision of the relevant national authorities, carried out by persons who are not subject to the general qualification rules of the public school teacher. No subject in public school can be run by a person without a higher education - the catechesis can run alumni of seminaries, and catechesis are not subject to the obligation of education and improvement related to professional promotion. If, as the supporters of the state paying catechists claim, they are teachers like everyone else, then why do we apply completely different rules to them than all other teachers? We know that there are two possible models of conducting such activities: confessional, such as in Poland, closely associated with the mission of forming believers, also present in countries such as Bulgaria - and then it seems natural that this confessional model should be financed by a religious union - and non-confessional, which does not serve a particular religion, teaching a specific Church, which has long evolved into classes devoted to value systems in Western Europe, and then is financed by the state, but is implementing a program set by the state. This model is in Finland, Denmark, Estonia. There are also intermediate models based on the agreement of the state with religious unions, e.g. in Germany or in the UK, where the programme, the appointment of teachers, the supervision of the implementation of the religious programme are common: states and interested religious unions in specific places. In Germany, as we know, there is still land diversity associated with this. We regard the funding of the confessional form of catechesis in a country that does not have a State Church as being governed by the Education Act, and this country is the Republic of Poland declaring impartiality in matters of worldview and the separation of the Church from the State as an inconsistency of the system. Let me draw your attention to a consequence of today's model of funding, namely the financing of confessional catechesis, where we do not have supervision of the curriculum and the way of teaching. A few days ago, the press described controversy about the organisation and financing of Islam teaching in Częstochowa. In view of what is currently happening in Europe, is this model of funding and not asking what is happening in the course of teaching ultimately beneficial? I would like to remind you that the principles contained in the Education System Act apply to all registered denominations in the Republic of Poland. Thanks to our action and the identification of the problem for the first time in years, the research studios of public opinion asked Poles in their polls about funding catechesis. According to them, depending on the survey, between 49% and 66% of Poles are of the opinion that the catechesis should be financed by ecclesiastical funds, it should not be financed by the state budget. Given the dominant percentage of Poles declaring to be believers, our postulate has found support in the group of believers. The majority of those who support our request, from simple arithmetic, are believers. This does not surprise us, because it is in line with the social teaching of the Church, which the absolute primacy of upbringing grants to parents and the Church. The declaration of Christian upbringing of the Second Vatican Council states: "Parents, because they gave life to children, are to the highest extent obliged to raise their offspring and must therefore be regarded as their first and principal educators. This educational task is so important that its possible absence could be replaced with difficulty. It is also about the role of the Church: "In a special way, the duty of education belongs to the Church not only because it should be regarded as a human society capable of carrying out an educational function, but, above all, because it has the task of showing all people the way of salvation, and to the believers giving the life of Christ and helping them with constant care, so that they may attain the fullness of this life. [[Very nice.]] In this document, you also have: (...) certain obligations and rights of the State, because it is up to you to organise what is required by the common temporal good. Its task is to support the various ways in which young people are raised, namely to protect their parents' responsibilities and rights and to help them, to take over, in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, education in the absence of initiative by parents and other communities. The citizens' draft law amending the Law on the Education System concerns a change in the content of the article. 12 Law of 7 September 1991 on the Education System. According to the current wording of the paragraph. 1 This article organizes religious education in public kindergartens, primary schools and secondary schools at the request of parents, public upper secondary schools at the request of parents or pupils, and, once they are old enough to learn religion, the students decide on their own. The conditions and manner in which religion and ethics are organised in public kindergartens and schools shall be determined on the basis of the authorisation provided for in Article. Article 12 (1) (a) and (b) of Regulation (EU) No 1303/2013 2 of the Law of 7 September 1991 on the Education System, Regulation of the Minister for National Education of 14 April 1992 on the conditions and organisation of religious education in public kindergartens and schools. The proposed change in the content of the paragraph in the citizens' project. Article 1 The 12th Law on the Education System consists of granting the right to decide to participate in religious education in upper secondary schools only to pupils, disregarding the rights of parents, and banning public funding for the organisation of religious education in public schools within the meaning of public finance regulations. Change in the lips, on the other hand. 2 of that Article consists in supplementing the provisions of that provision with the authorisation to issue a regulation laying down the conditions and the manner in which schools perform the tasks related to organising religious education in order to determine how to settle and cover the costs involved. The explanatory memorandum of the project refers to the article. 12 Concordat between the Holy See and Poland, signed in Warsaw on 28 July 1993. According to the authors of the project, Poland committed itself to organizing religious lessons in schools, but should not finance them in the name of the principle of worldview impartiality of countries resulting from the article. The Commission shall adopt implementing acts laying down detailed rules for the implementation of this Regulation in accordance with Regulation (EU) No 182/2011 of the European Parliament and of the Council laying down detailed rules for the implementation of this Regulation. 2 The Constitution of the Republic of Poland of 2 April 1997. The authors of the project did not take into account the fact that the provisions of the Art. The 12th Law of 7 September 1991 on the system of education is the basis for organizing, at the request of parents, the teachings of religions of many Christian denominations and non-Christian religions which are not subject to the provisions of the concordat, so the proposed ban on financing religious activities organised in schools and inter-school groups would apply to all churches and other religious associations with a regulated legal situation, currently teaching - in the number of about 30 - or planning to organise such activities in the future. Referring to the draft ban on public funding of religious activities, it should be noted that the introduction of the principle of funding these activities by the Churches or other religious associations, which is practically by parents who are members of these religious communities, would violate two constitutional principles: the principle of freedom of religion and the principle of free learning. In the light of Art. Article 53 (1) (b) and (c) of Regulation (EU) No 1303/2013 1 and 2 of the Constitution of the Republic of Poland The teaching of religion is an immanent component of the fundamental freedom of religion guaranteed by the Act. This principle is complemented by the regulation contained in Article. Article 53 (1) (b) and (c) of Regulation (EU) No 1303/2013 4 of the Constitution, stating that the religion of the Church or other religious union having a regulated legal situation may be the subject of teaching at school, and that freedom of conscience and religion of others must not be affected. The guarantee of freedom of religion is unconditional in the sense that the State cannot introduce non-constitutional conditions for the use of its forms set out in Article. Article 53 (1) (b) and (c) of Regulation (EU) No 1303/2013 2 constitutions that would restrict this freedom, e.g. introduce remunerations for participating in the teaching of religion organised in a public school. In connection with the regulation contained in Article. Article 53 (1) (b) and (c) of Regulation (EU) No 1303/2013 4 The Constitution of the Republic of Poland should be indicated in its Article. OJ L 347, 20.12.2013, p. 671. 2, which lays down the principle of free teaching in public schools, allowing only one exception from it, namely the provision in the Act of the ordinary possibility of collecting fees for the provision of certain educational services by public higher education institutions. It should also be noted that teachers in public kindergartens and schools are employed under the Act of 26 January 1982. A person conducting religious lessons, whether a clergyman or a secular person, is a teacher of this kindergarten or school. Therefore, a religious teacher remains in the relationship of working with an individual - a kindergarten or a school - who employs him. According to the definition of employment relationship, the employee undertakes to do a specific job for the employer and under his/her direction and at the place and time appointed by the employer, and the employer undertakes to employ the employee for remuneration. It is not possible for an employee to provide work without an equivalent remuneration paid by the employer. In most European countries, the teaching of religion organized at school, in a formula and in accordance with the principles of the educational system of a given country is financed by public funds: from the state budget or from the local government, depending on the specifics of the individual countries. In addition, in 28 countries 24 they finance religious lessons, in a way that the Polish Church could dream of. Proposal by the authors of the bill... I am at the disposal of strongly commenting volunteers for whom I have expressed my respect, I hope, with reciprocity, as regards detailed information on how this issue is resolved in individual European countries. The proposal of the authors of the bill concerning the organisation of religious and ethics lessons in upper secondary schools only at the request of pupils, justified by the claim that they are mature enough, raises concerns due to a significant violation of the rights of parents. It should therefore be noted that the provision of the Article. 48 (1) (a) 1, linked to Article. Article 53 (1) (b) and (c) of Regulation (EU) No 1303/2013 3 The Constitution of the Republic of Poland guarantees parents the right to raise children according to their own beliefs. The rights of parents do not deprive their children of the right to exercise freedom of conscience and religion and to have their own beliefs, to the extent that they are mature. Parents should take into account the child's maturity and freedom of conscience and belief in their educational role, which does not mean that they are in breach of their parental rights. Regulation contained in Article. The 12th Act of 7 September 1991 on the system of education serves to create proper relations within the family, creating a field for dialogue and broadening the possibilities for the child to participate in decision-making as his maturity deepens. Constitutional guarantees to respect the rights of both parents and pupils are in favour of a negative assessment of the proposed change as an unauthorised introduction of a statutory restriction on the rights of parents of a minor child in matters relating to the worldview. In summary, the government has expressed a negative view of the proposals for amendments to the citizens' draft law amending the law on the education system. I will be pleased and attentive to follow the discussion, hoping for a serious and substantive speech, serving you and answering your questions. Thank you very much. [[Applause]]

ParlaMint-PL_2016-01-28-sejm-09-2.u327	Dear Marshal! Minister! High Izbo! Dear Applicants! On behalf of the Polish People's Party Parliamentary Club, I am pleased to present my position on the citizens' draft law amending the Act on the Education System, print No 48. Ladies and gentlemen, the project assumes that public kindergartens, primary schools and junior high schools will organise religious education at the request of their parents and public upper secondary schools at the request of the students themselves. The most important change in the draft legislative initiative prepared by the Committee is the financing of religious lessons. The Act contains a provision that assumes that it will not be possible to finance them in part or entirely from public funds within the meaning of public finance legislation. According to the project, the Minister responsible for education and upbringing in agreement with the authorities of the Catholic Church and the Polish Autokephalic Orthodox Church and other Churches and religious associations will define by regulation the conditions and manner in which schools perform tasks, as well as the way in which they settle and cover the costs associated with the organisation of religious education. Today, according to the Act on the Education System, the teaching of religion in public kindergartens, elementary schools and junior high schools takes place at the request of parents, at least in theory, and in upper secondary schools - at the request of parents or students themselves. According to the decree of the Minister of National Education, parents can choose whether the child will attend ethics, religion, or not to attend one or another. These provisions apply to all churches and religious associations. Ladies and gentlemen, approximately PLN 1365 million per year is spent on salaries for religious teachers from the state budget. With regard to who would cover these costs, the project foresees that the Minister for Education should deal with this in a separate regulation. But it is obvious, ladies and gentlemen, that these should be religious unions or, if they are not interested, parents directly. Ladies and gentlemen, parents are waiting for the 500 zlotys promised to be paid from January. We can't charge them another fee. Therefore, the Parliamentary Club of the Polish People's Party does not accept such solutions, and therefore does not support the citizens' draft law on amending the law on the system of education. Thank you very much. [[Don't back it up, don't throw it away.]]

We proceed with the examination of item 15 of the agenda: Report of the Rules of Procedure and the Messenger's Affairs on the application of the Chief Police Commissioner of 17 November 2015 to the Sejm's consent to the criminal liability of Mr Jacek Żalek (print No 188). ParlaMint-PL_2016-01-28-sejm-09-2.u456	Mrs. Marshal! High Izbo! I have the honour to present the report of the Rules of Procedure and Parliamentary Affairs Committee, in print No 188, on the case of Mr Jacek Żalk. Mrs. Marshal! High Izbo! The Chief Police Commissioner, through the Attorney General, asked the Marshal of the Sejm to agree to be held responsible for the offence of Mr Jacek Żalka in the case of an act committed on 21 June 2015. The Rules of Procedure and Parliamentary Affairs Committee of 13 January 2016 following a debate, and earlier after hearing the position of the chief police chief's representative and Mr Jacek Żalka, after I said the debate, decided to recommend that the High House reject the application of the Chief Police Commissioner to give his consent to the criminal liability of Mr Jacek Żalk. Thank you very much. [[Applause]]
ParlaMint-PL_2016-01-28-sejm-09-2.u457	Thank you very much, sir. Let me remind you that the Sejm according to the article. Article 133 (1) (b) and (c) of Regulation (EU) No 1303/2013 6 of the Rules of Procedure of the Sejm shall examine the committee's report by listening only to the rapporteur. The Member concerned shall have the right to speak. This report shall not be discussed. As I do not see Mr Jack Żalk, I inform you that we will proceed to the vote on this subject at the next session of the Sejm. On this we have exhausted the agenda of the 9th meeting of the Polish Sejm.
